| Fixed income / bond | FGN and corporate bonds | Two-to-five-year money |
| Equity | Nigerian shares | Long-term growth, 5+ years |
| Balanced | Shares, bonds, cash | One decision for a cautious investor |
| Dollar / Eurobond | FGN and corporate Eurobonds | Currency protection |
| Ethical / Shariah | Screened equities, sukuk | Non-interest requirement |
| REIT / real estate | Income-producing property | Property without a landlord's life |

**The cost, which is quiet by design.** A management fee of 1%–2% annually, deducted from the fund rather than billed to you. You never watch it leave, which is precisely why people ignore it. Ask for the **total expense ratio**. Over twenty years, the difference between 1% and 2.5% a year is not a detail; it is a large fraction of your result, transferred by arithmetic rather than by decision.

---

**Now the index question**, where I must correct the advice that has been imported wholesale into Nigeria without inspection.

The global finding is powerful and true: most active managers fail to beat their index after fees, and the ones who win in one period are largely not the ones who win in the next. Buying the index costs less, and lower cost is the one edge available to everybody.

**But look at what "the index" means here.** The NGX All-Share Index is dominated by a handful of very large companies, several with a small **free float** — only a fraction of their shares actually trade, because a parent or founder holds the rest. So:

1. **You get much less diversification than the word implies.** A few boardrooms move the whole number.
2. **The index can be moved by relatively little money**, since a small float on a giant weight can lift the entire ASI. A rising headline does not always mean broad prosperity. **Check market breadth** — how many shares rose versus fell — before believing the number.
3. **It is a narrow slice of the actual economy.** Informal trade, most agriculture, most technology, the entire vast unlisted middle of Nigerian business — none of it is there. "Buy Nigeria" on the NGX buys banks, cement, telecoms and consumer goods.

**The ETFs listed** include the Vetiva Griffin 30, a Stanbic IBTC ETF 30, the Lotus Halal Equity ETF, some Meristem value and growth vehicles, and **NewGold**, backed by physical gold. Fees are lower than active equity funds. **Check traded volumes before committing money you may need back: a cheap fund you cannot exit is not cheap.**

**My synthesis**, which differs from the imported advice in one respect: use a local ETF or low-cost fund as the **core** of your Nigerian equity exposure, and then get your real diversification from **asset classes and currencies** rather than from adding a fifth bank share. In a country where the index is four companies wearing a trench coat, the diversification argument for indexing is weaker than in America — but the *cost* argument is unchanged, and the cost argument was always the stronger one.

**REITs** deserve a paragraph, since they solve a real problem. A Real Estate Investment Trust owns income-producing property and distributes the rent; you buy units like a share, with no title risk, no tenants, no omo onile. The Nigerian reality: the market is small — a handful of vehicles — and they trade thinly, with wide spreads and prices that can sit far from the value of the underlying buildings. A genuine option, not yet a deep one.

**Three ways to own Nigerian shares**, and the honest trade-offs:

| | Direct shares | Equity mutual fund | ETF |
|---|---|---|---|
| Minimum | Price of one board lot | ₦5,000–₦10,000 | Price of a unit |
| Cost | ~2–3% round trip, then nothing | 1%–2% a year, every year | Usually well under 1% a year, plus trading costs |
| Effort | Annual reports, monitoring, decisions | None | None |
| Diversification | Only what you build | Immediate | Immediate, but concentrated by the index |
| Liquidity | Depends entirely on the share | Redeem with the manager | Depends on the ETF's traded volume |
| Where held | Your name at CSCS | Units with the manager | Your name at CSCS |
| Main risk | You pick badly and cannot exit | Fees compound against you | You inherit the index's concentration |

*If you will not read annual reports, the second and third columns are not a lesser path — they are the correct one.*

**How to compare any fund, in order:** (1) is it on the SEC register — checked by you; (2) one-, three- and five-year returns **against the right benchmark** — a fund returning 30% when the index returned 40% underperformed, however good 30% sounds; (3) the total expense ratio; (4) what it actually holds — two funds with the same label can be different animals, and you may be buying the same five bank shares in three funds; (5) minimums, redemption time, penalties.

**What to do.**

1. **If you will not read annual reports, buy funds.** This is not a lesser path. The investor who honestly knows he will not do the work and buys a diversified fund will beat the one who buys individual shares and does not do the work.
2. **Ask for the total expense ratio in writing** before investing in any fund.
3. **Never judge a fund on one good year**, and never on a chart whose starting date was chosen by the person showing it to you.

**In plain words:** If you will not read company reports, buy a fund instead. A fund is many people's money managed by a professional, and ₦5,000 or ₦10,000 gets you in. Check the fee, because a fee is charged every year whether the fund does well or badly. And check that you can sell it when you need to.

**If you remember one thing:** if you will not read annual reports, buy a low-cost diversified fund and stop pretending — and check the traded volume of anything you may one day need to sell.

**In Pidgin:** If you no go read company report, buy fund. No shame dey there — na the correct answer.
## 24. Your pension is quietly owning you

> *Ọwọ́ ọmọdé kò tó pẹpẹ, ti àgbàlagbà kò wọ akèngbè.* — **Yoruba:** the child's hand cannot reach the shelf, and the elder's hand will not enter the gourd. Neither is complete alone.

If you work in the formal sector, you already own a large, professionally managed, regulated portfolio. There is a good chance you have never looked at it. For many Nigerians it becomes the largest financial asset of their lives, accumulated entirely by inattention — which is, I note without irony, **the most successful investment strategy most people will ever execute.**

There is a lesson in that, and it is not flattering to the financial industry. The money you could not touch, could not panic out of, could not switch on a headline, and could not be talked into moving by a man with a laminated brochure — that is the money that grew. **Illiquidity, which we normally treat as a defect, functioned here as protection against yourself.**

**The machinery.** Under the Contributory Pension Scheme: you contribute a minimum of 8% of emoluments, your employer at least 10%. It goes into your **Retirement Savings Account (RSA)**, which is yours and follows you between jobs. A **Pension Fund Administrator (PFA)** invests it; a separate **Pension Fund Custodian** holds the assets, so the PFA never touches the money directly. **PenCom** regulates. The separation of administrator from custodian is the whole safety architecture, and it is the same principle that makes a regulated mutual fund a different species from your cousin's arrangement.

**Check first, before anything else in this chapter:** employers sometimes deduct the 8% and fail to remit it. This is illegal and PenCom takes complaints. Log in and verify that money arrived every month for the last six months, and that the amount is right. **An unremitted pension is a hole that no later cleverness fills.**

**The multi-fund structure**, which is not advertised and which compounds silently over a career:

| Fund | For whom | Character |
|---|---|---|
| **I** | Under 50, **must opt in** | Most aggressive; highest equity allowance |
| **II** | **Default** under 50 | Balanced |
| **III** | **Default** 50 and above | Conservative |
| **IV** | Retirees | Income focused |
| **V** | Micro Pension — informal sector | Flexible contributions |
| **VI** | Non-interest (Shariah), active and retiree | Screened |

**The decision.** A thirty-year-old sitting in default Fund II for an entire career holds a portfolio more cautious than his horizon warrants. He has the one thing that makes equity risk survivable — decades in which to recover — and he is not using it. **Fund I is opt-in. Nobody will move you. You must ask.**

I will not pretend this is free: Fund I falls harder in a bad year, and if that would make you switch at the bottom, it is not for you. Read the mandate and the published returns. But **make an actual choice** rather than drifting into a default you did not know was a default.

**You may transfer PFAs** once a year through the RSA Transfer System. If your PFA lags its peers over three to five years — the returns are published — move.

**Micro Pension (Fund V)** is for the majority of working Nigerians who are not in formal employment: traders, tailors, mechanics, drivers, hairdressers, farmers, freelancers. You open an RSA and contribute whatever you can, whenever you can, with a portion available for contingent withdrawal before retirement. It is regulated, custodied, and invested in the same instruments as formal-sector money. **It is barely marketed, and take-up has been a small fraction of what it could be**, which is a shame of a specific and quantifiable kind.

**Access before retirement**, the routes that exist: loss of employment (historically 25% after four months unemployed); **residential mortgage equity** — PenCom's framework allows eligible contributors to apply a portion of the RSA balance, historically up to 25%, as equity contribution for a residential mortgage, which for many salaried Nigerians is the missing piece that makes a house possible and which is not widely known; medical incapacitation; and death.

**And two tax points**, since I have complained about tax elsewhere and should acknowledge advantage where it exists: pension contributions reduce taxable income going in, growth is untaxed inside the fund, and compliant retirement benefits are generally exempt. **That is a three-way advantage no other Nigerian product offers**, and it is a strong argument for taking the RSA seriously rather than treating it as a deduction that happens to you.

**At retirement** you choose between **programmed withdrawal** (periodic payments, balance stays invested, remainder passes to your heirs) and a **life annuity** from a licensed insurer (guaranteed income for life, however long you live, but typically nothing left over). The trade: flexibility and inheritance against the risk of outliving the money. There is no universal answer; it depends on your health, your other assets and your family.

**One planning point that is routinely missed.** Retirement is a start date. A sixty-year-old Nigerian may live twenty-five more years, and at 20% inflation prices double roughly every four years. **A retirement portfolio entirely in fixed income is not safe; it is a slow, certain loss**, and its safety is an illusion produced by looking at the naira figure rather than what the naira buys.

**What to do.**

**The law on this.** The **Pension Reform Act 2014** governs all of it. An employer with the defined minimum number of employees **must** deduct 8% from you and contribute at least 10%, and remit both to your RSA within a set number of days. **Deducting your contribution and failing to remit it is an offence**, and PenCom appoints recovery agents who have collected substantial sums with penalties. Your PFA administers the money but a separate **custodian** holds it, which is why a failing PFA does not put your balance at risk. Employers must also maintain **group life insurance** for you. Complain to **PenCom**.

1. **Log in today.** Confirm balance, six months of remittances, which fund you are in, and that your next-of-kin details are current.
2. **If under 40, read the Fund I mandate** and make a deliberate choice.
3. **If self-employed, open a Micro Pension account.** You have no employer doing this for you, and you have no HR department to blame later.


**In plain words:** If you work for a company, money is already being put aside for your retirement, and it may be the biggest money you own. Log in and check it is really arriving. Choose your fund deliberately — younger people can ask for the more aggressive one. If you work for yourself, open a Micro Pension account; nobody will do it for you.

**If you remember one thing:** log in, confirm six months of remittances, and choose your fund deliberately: this is probably your largest asset, and it grew precisely because you could not touch it.

**In Pidgin:** Your pension fit be your biggest money and you never even open the app. Log in today, check say dem dey remit.
## 25. Insurance is a bet you want to lose

> *Amana riba ce.* — **Hausa:** trust is profit.

Insurance is the only financial product where the correct outcome is that you receive nothing. This confuses people so thoroughly that a whole country under-buys it.

Let me put it in the language of this book. **You are paying a small, certain, known cost to remove a large, uncertain, unbearable one.** You are converting an open-ended exposure into a fixed fee. In Chapter 6's terms, you are accepting Type Two error — paying for protection you may not need — in order to eliminate Type One error, the one that ends you.

> A year in which you claimed nothing is a year in which nothing terrible happened. That is not a wasted premium. That is the product working.

**And insurance is not an investment.** When a salesman blends the two — "you get your money back at maturity!" — he has bundled a bet you want to lose with a savings account, and the bundling exists mainly so that neither half can be priced by you. Separate them.

**The honest Nigerian context**, since I am not going to pretend: insurance penetration here is very low, and not irrationally. There is a history of denied claims, aggressive small print, and undercapitalised insurers. This has improved considerably under NAICOM's recapitalisation and enforcement, and there are solid companies operating today. But it means you must **choose carefully rather than cheaply**, which is the reverse of how most people buy insurance.

**Health, first and before any investment.** The most common cause of financial ruin for a Nigerian middle-class family is not a bad investment. It is an uninsured medical event, paid out of pocket, plus what the extended family sends — so one event impoverishes fifteen people. Options: an employer HMO scheme (find out exactly what it covers and what happens when you leave the job); a private HMO plan (compare the hospital network, the exclusions, the annual limits and the authorisation process, which matters enormously in an emergency); or your **state scheme under the NHIA**, which made health insurance mandatory in 2022, with several states running genuinely affordable schemes.

**Life insurance, if anyone depends on your income.** Buy **term** — pure protection, fixed period, pays out if you die within it, nothing if you do not. It is remarkably cheap for a healthy young person, precisely because the annual probability is small. Avoid bundled "investment-linked" products in most cases; buy cheap cover and invest the difference where you can see what you earn and what you pay.

Two exceptions I will grant: an endowment can enforce discipline on someone who genuinely cannot save otherwise, and some education plans carry a **waiver-of-premium** feature — if the parent dies, the insurer keeps funding the plan and the child's education continues. That is a real and valuable option; judge it on its specific terms rather than on the salesman's enthusiasm.

**Term versus the bundled products**, stated as plainly as I can:

| | Term life | Endowment / investment-linked |
|---|---|---|
| What you buy | Protection only | Protection plus a savings pot |
| Cost for the same cover | Low | Several times higher |
| Payout if you survive | Nothing | The maturity value |
| Can you see what each half costs? | Yes — there is only one half | Rarely, and that is largely the point |
| Effect of stopping early | Cover ends | Often a surrender value well below what you paid |
| Who it genuinely suits | Almost everyone with dependants | Someone who truly cannot save any other way, or who needs a waiver-of-premium education plan |

*Buy term, invest the difference where you can see the fees. The exceptions are real but they are exceptions.*

**How much cover?** Enough to clear your debts plus five to ten times annual income, adjusted for how long dependants need support and what school fees remain.

**Two things determine whether it ever works:** name your beneficiaries clearly **and tell them the policy exists** — a policy nobody knows about pays nobody — and **disclose honestly on the application.** Non-disclosure is the commonest reason claims fail, and the failure lands on your family at the worst possible moment.

**Group life cover** is required of employers under the Pension Reform Act, typically three times annual emoluments. Check yours is in force and your next of kin is right.

**Motor:** third party is the legal minimum and covers only damage you cause others; comprehensive covers your vehicle too. **Fake certificates are widespread in Nigeria — verify on the NIID database.** A fake certificate means you paid money and have no cover, discoverable only at the accident.

**The compulsory covers**, frequently ignored: third-party motor; group life; employers' liability; buildings under construction; occupiers' liability for public buildings; professional indemnity for certain professions.

**Property:** fire and special perils on building and contents, with flood, storm and burglary as extensions. **Insure for current rebuilding cost, not what you paid in 2016** — underinsurance triggers proportional reduction of claims, which is a nasty surprise delivered at the worst time. For businesses, add goods-in-transit, burglary, fidelity guarantee, public liability, marine cargo for importers, and **business interruption**, which covers lost profit while you rebuild and is the cover businesses most often wish they had bought.

**Getting a claim paid** — where Nigerian disputes actually originate:

1. **Choose a strong insurer, not the cheapest quote.** Check NAICOM's list; ask people in your industry who has actually paid them.
2. **Use a licensed broker** for anything complex. Paid by the insurer, costs you nothing extra, and their job at claim time is to fight for you.
3. **Read the exclusions before the benefits**, then the conditions, especially notification deadlines — sometimes 48 hours.
4. **Document everything at the scene.** Photographs, police report, medical reports, receipts, witnesses. More than you think you need.
5. **Keep a written record** of every call and follow up in writing.
6. **If unfairly denied, escalate** — the insurer's internal process, then **NAICOM's complaints bureau**, then the courts. Many Nigerians stop at the first rejection letter. A well-documented, properly disclosed claim escalated to NAICOM is often paid.

**What to do.**

**The law on this.** Insurers are licensed by **NAICOM** under the Insurance Act, and health insurance sits under the **National Health Insurance Authority Act 2022**, which made health insurance **mandatory** for all Nigerians and legal residents and created state schemes. Several insurances are compulsory by law: third-party motor, group life for employers, employers' liability, buildings under construction, occupiers' liability for public buildings, and professional indemnity for some professions. Motor cover can be verified on the **NIID** database — fake certificates are widespread. If a claim is unfairly refused, use the insurer's internal process and then **NAICOM's complaints bureau**.

1. **Buy health cover this month**, before any investment product.
2. **If anyone depends on you, get three term life quotes** and buy one.
3. **Put every policy in one folder** with the schedule, premium receipts, broker's contact and claims line — and tell your spouse where it is. The claim your family cannot find is the claim that is never made.


**In plain words:** Insurance is the one thing you buy hoping never to use. A year with no claim is a good year, not wasted money. Buy health cover before any investment, because one hospital admission can wipe out ten years of saving. Buy simple term life if people depend on your income. And read the exclusions before the benefits — that is where arguments come from.

**If you remember one thing:** a year with no claim is the product working — buy health cover before any investment, and read the exclusions before the benefits.

**In Pidgin:** Year wey you no claim anything na better year. Buy health cover before you buy any investment.
## 26. Land does not depreciate, but your title may not exist

The most repeated investment sentence in Nigeria is half true, which is the most dangerous proportion.

**What is true:** land in the right corridor has produced spectacular returns. Lekki, Ajah, Ibeju-Lekki, parts of Abuja and Port Harcourt made real money for people who bought early and held. It has been one of the few genuine inflation hedges accessible to people with no capital-market access, and I will not patronise a country that has been right about this for fifty years.

**What the sentence conceals:**

- **The land does not depreciate; your title may turn out never to have existed.** Government acquisition, family land sold to four buyers, forged documents, "excision in progress" that remains in progress for a decade.
- **Omo onile levies are recurring, not a one-off** — foundation levy, block levy, roofing levy. A real, unbudgeted development tax.
- **It is illiquid.** Try selling a plot in a month and you will learn that the market price and the achievable price are different numbers. Illiquidity is not a minor inconvenience; it is the reason a family with a ₦40 million plot borrows at 40% for a ₦2 million hospital bill.
- **Bare land has negative carry.** It pays nothing while you pay survey, fencing, security and land use charge.
- **The advertised return is never net.** Add agency (around 5%), legal (5%–10%), survey, deed, stamp duty, registration, and **Governor's Consent**, which is frequently the largest surprise. **Perfection costs of 10%–20% on top of purchase are normal.** A "40% gain in three years" with 15% of costs and three years of carrying charges is a different and much smaller animal.

**The legal foundation, briefly, because everything follows from it.** Under the **Land Use Act 1978, all land in a state is vested in the Governor**, who holds it in trust. You do not own land outright; you hold a **right of occupancy**, generally 99 years.

Therefore:

- **Certificate of Occupancy (C of O)** — the state's formal grant. The strongest ordinary title. Verify at the registry; forgeries exist.
- **Governor's Consent** — and here is the point most buyers miss: **any transfer requires the Governor's consent to be valid.** If the C of O is in the seller's name and you buy, you do not inherit it; you must obtain Consent to the assignment. Skipping this is the commonest defect in Nigerian property ownership, and people postpone it until they try to sell or mortgage and discover they cannot.
- **Deed of Assignment** — the transfer contract; must be executed, stamped, registered, then perfected with Consent.
- **Excision and gazette** — where government releases land back to original families. Ask for the **gazette number** and verify it. "Excision in progress" means it has not happened.
- **Survey plan and charting** at the Surveyor-General's office — tells you whether the land sits in a government acquisition, a road setback or a right of way. Cheap, and it catches disasters.

**Due diligence, in order, and pay nothing before step five:**

1. Verify the seller's identity and **authority** to sell — especially for family land, where the branch that did not sign will appear later.
2. Collect all title documents.
3. **Instruct your own lawyer** — not the seller's, not the agent's recommendation.
4. **Official search at the state land registry**, result in writing: registered owner, encumbrances, acquisition status.
5. **Charting at the Surveyor-General's office.**
6. **Physical inspection, twice, including in the rainy season.** Talk to the neighbours and ask directly who else claims this land. They will tell you, and they have no incentive to lie to you.
7. **Establish the community position in writing** with recognised representatives.
8. Structure payments against document delivery; never cash to an individual without documentation.
9. Execute, stamp, register, and **start the Consent application immediately** — not "later."
10. Keep the entire file forever, scanned, where your family can find it.

**On mortgages**, briefly, because the good option is obscure: commercial bank mortgages exist at commercial rates and require substantial equity. The **National Housing Fund**, through the Federal Mortgage Bank and a Primary Mortgage Bank, lends at a **concessionary rate well below market** to contributors — constrained by maximum loan size and processing, but genuinely cheap money, which as Chapter 8 explained is a rare and valuable thing in an inflationary country. Combine it with the pension equity route in Chapter 24 and a salaried Nigerian has an actual path to a house. Also check whether your workplace **cooperative** lends for housing; it is frequently the cheapest money available to a salaried person anywhere in the country.

**And the low-capital routes**, for those who should not put everything into one plot: REITs and real estate funds; **fractional platforms** — where you must ask whether the operator is SEC-registered, what your legal ownership actually is, whether the return is "guaranteed" (which would mean you are lending to a company, not owning property), who holds title, and what the exit is; **cooperative land purchase** with a lawyer-drafted agreement covering who holds title and what happens when a member stops paying; and simply **improving what you already own**, which requires no new title at all.

**Short-lets**, since everyone asks: gross yields can be several times annual rent, and it is a business, not a passive investment — furnishing, power (a Nigerian short-let without reliable power is destroyed in reviews), water, internet, cleaning, security, platform commission, and a manager. **Model 50%–60% occupancy, not 90%.** And check the estate rules and local regulations: many estates prohibit short-lets outright, which is a risk to the entire business model of a single property.

### Case: The plot that could not be sold

*Composite; names invented.*

In 2014 **the Adeyemis** bought two plots on the Lagos–Ibadan corridor for ₦1,800,000 from a family that produced a receipt, a hand-drawn survey and a "deed" signed by three men described as the family heads. The price was roughly a third of what an estate two kilometres away was charging, which they took as evidence of a good relationship rather than a warning.

They used the seller's lawyer, because he was there and it was cheaper. They did not conduct a registry search. They did not chart the survey at the Surveyor-General's office. They fenced one plot; the other stayed bare.

For eight years the paper value rose. At family gatherings the land was mentioned. It was, in the household's own accounting, the family's principal asset.

In 2022 they tried to sell, to fund their daughter's postgraduate course abroad. Three things emerged in six weeks. A branch of the seller's family had not consented and had sold the same parcel to a second buyer in 2017. Part of one plot fell within a state acquisition that a charting in 2014 would have revealed for a small fee. And having no registrable title, they had never obtained Governor's Consent, so they had nothing to assign even if a buyer had been willing.

The daughter deferred a year, then did not go. Litigation was still unresolved four years later. The neighbouring estate's plots, bought at three times the price with a C of O and perfected title, had been sold twice in the same period.

**What actually separated the Adeyemis from the family two kilometres away:** roughly ₦250,000 and one afternoon, in 2014 — an independent lawyer, a registry search and a charting. **They did not buy the wrong land. They bought land they never legally owned**, and the eight years of appreciation were appreciation of somebody else's asset.

**What to do.**

**The law on this.** Under the **Land Use Act 1978** all land in a state is vested in the Governor, and what you buy is a **right of occupancy**, usually for 99 years. Two consequences decide everything: **any transfer requires the Governor's consent to be valid**, so a deed alone does not complete your ownership; and an **unregistered instrument** may not be admissible as evidence of title, so registration is not optional. Government may revoke for overriding public interest, with compensation for improvements — which is why land under acquisition is worthless whatever the seller says. Chapter 77 sets out the tenancy rules if you are renting.

1. **Never buy land without an independent lawyer, a registry search and a charting.** The three together cost a small fraction of the purchase and remove most of the ways this goes wrong.
2. **Budget perfection costs before you commit**, including Governor's Consent, and recompute your expected return with them included.
3. **Do not let property become more than half your net worth**, however solid it feels. Solid and safe are different words. You cannot sell a bedroom in an emergency.


**In plain words:** Land does not lose value, but a title you never checked can leave you owning nothing. Use your own lawyer, not the seller's. Do a search at the land registry and a charting at the surveyor's office. And remember that any transfer needs the Governor's consent to be complete, plus other costs that add ten to twenty per cent on top of the price.

**If you remember one thing:** land does not depreciate, but an unverified title and an unobtained Governor's Consent can leave you owning a receipt; hire your own lawyer, always.

**In Pidgin:** Land no dey depreciate, but paper wey no correct fit make you own nothing. Use your own lawyer, do search, do charting.
## 27. Dollars: the second currency of a one-currency mind

I have kept this chapter short because the argument is arithmetic, and arithmetic does not need adjectives.

Take January 2023. Two people, ₦10,000,000 each, when the rate was around ₦460 to the dollar.

**Person A** buys a naira money market fund and earns an excellent 15% for the year: **₦11,500,000**. He is pleased. His statement is pleasing.

By year end the rate was around ₦900. In dollars: he started with about **$21,700** and ended with about **$12,800**.

**He earned 15% and lost roughly 41% of his global purchasing power.**

**Person B** bought a dollar Eurobond fund yielding a boring 5%. $21,700 became about **$22,800** — which at year-end rates was over **₦20,000,000**.

> A 5% dollar return is not worse than a 15% naira return. They are quoted in different units. Comparing them without converting is the most common analytical error in Nigerian personal finance, and it is committed daily by people who can do calculus.

**Why this matters even if you never leave Nigeria:** because Nigeria imports fuel, medicine, phones, laptops, cars and parts, wheat, machinery, and the education of your children abroad. When the naira falls, the naira price of all of it rises. **The exchange rate is not a foreign concern; it is an engine of your domestic cost of living.**

**The routes, concretely:**

| Route | Minimum | What it gives |
|---|---|---|
| Domiciliary account | Any | Dollars, essentially no yield |
| Nigerian dollar/Eurobond fund | Often around $1,000 | Managed dollar bonds, dollar return |
| Nigerian apps offering foreign markets | Small | US shares and ETFs |
| Global ETF via international broker | Varies | The world, in one instrument |

**FGN Eurobonds** direct usually require $100,000 or $200,000, which is why funds exist.

**I deliberately do not name the apps.** They appear, merge, change regulatory status and disappear faster than any book can track, and a reader who trusts a name in print two years after publication has been badly served. Learn the evaluation instead — it will still work when every current platform is gone.

**Seven questions to ask any platform before it holds your money:**

1. **Is it registered with the Nigerian SEC for what it is actually doing** — and can you find the registration yourself, on the SEC's own site, rather than on the platform's marketing page? The rules covering these platforms have shifted more than once; a registration from three years ago is not a current answer.
2. **Where are the assets actually held, and by whom?** A named third-party custodian or a foreign broker holding securities in your name is a different proposition from an entity that simply records a number in an app.
3. **Under which regulator does the underlying account sit?** Nigerian, US, or somewhere else — and what protection does that regulator provide if the platform fails rather than if the market falls?
4. **What is the total cost of getting money in?** Add the embedded exchange rate (compare it to the rate you could get elsewhere on the same day), card fees, transfer fees, and any deposit charge. Several percent is common and it is disclosed nowhere in one place.
5. **What is the total cost of getting money out, and how long does it take?** Test with a small withdrawal early, exactly as in Chapter 14.
6. **What happens to my holdings if the company closes?** If nobody can answer this plainly, that is the answer.
7. **Who owns and runs it, and what happens to them if it fails?** Chapter 11.

**And one technical trap that is genuinely obscure: US estate tax.** Non-resident foreigners holding US-situated assets, including US-listed shares and US-domiciled ETFs, above a low threshold can face US estate tax exposure on death — at rates that would shock the family discovering it. Many international investors therefore hold **Ireland-domiciled UCITS ETFs** tracking the same indices instead, which also generally improves the dividend withholding position. If your foreign holdings pass a few thousand dollars, this is worth an hour with an adviser.

**How to size it without gambling**, which is the actual question:

1. **Match currency to liability.** Naira for rent, local fees, food, land. Hard currency for a foreign degree, relocation, imported inventory, treatment abroad. **When you do this, the dollar asset is not an investment; it is insurance, and its low yield is the premium.** That reframing dissolves most of the anxiety about "wasting" money on a 5% return.
2. **Hold a standing allocation regardless** — for most middle-class Nigerians somewhere in the 20%–40% range ensures no single devaluation halves the family's net worth. Choose the number, write it down, rebalance annually, stop watching.
3. **Do not chase the rate.** Buying in a panic at the top of a spike and selling in frustration on the retracement is how people lose money while being right about the decade.
4. **Never earn naira and owe dollars.** That mismatch destroyed real Nigerian companies in 2023–24 and it will do it again.

### Case: Two savings accounts, June 2023

*Composite; names invented.*

Both men were engineers at the same firm in Port Harcourt. Both had saved for four years toward a master's degree in the United Kingdom, and both intended to start in September 2024. Both had, in January 2023, almost exactly ₦18,000,000.

**Chidi** kept everything in a naira money market fund. It was the responsible choice by every measure he had been taught: SEC-registered, liquid, earning about 14% that year. He had considered a domiciliary account and concluded that a 4% dollar return was inferior to 14%, which is arithmetic, and which is the error.

**Segun** had opened a domiciliary account in 2021 for no strategic reason — a client had once paid him in dollars — and had drifted into converting roughly half of each year's savings, holding about $19,000 and ₦9,000,000 by January 2023.

At the January 2023 rate of about ₦460, Chidi's ₦18,000,000 was about $39,000. The programme, with living costs, needed about $45,000.

By September 2024 Chidi's fund had grown to roughly ₦23,000,000. In naira, he had never had more. Converted at the prevailing rate, it was under $16,000. **He had earned a positive return every single month and lost about 60% of the thing he was actually saving for.** He did not go.

Segun's dollars were still $19,000; his naira half had grown to about ₦11,500,000, worth roughly $7,500. He had about $26,500 — also short, but recoverable with a year's additional saving and a partial scholarship. He started in 2025.

**What separated them was not skill, information or timing.** Neither predicted anything; Segun's domiciliary account was an accident he never rationalised. What separated them is that one man's savings were denominated in the currency of his goal and the other's were not.

**The lesson is not "buy dollars."** It is the rule in this chapter: **match the currency to the liability.** Chidi's obligation was in pounds and dollars; every naira of yield he earned was compensation for a risk he had not realised he was taking.

**What to do.**

**The law on this.** Foreign exchange is regulated by the **CBN** under the Foreign Exchange (Monitoring and Miscellaneous Provisions) Act, and the rules on what you may hold, transfer and receive have changed repeatedly — check the current circulars rather than relying on what was true two years ago. Money transfer operators must be CBN-licensed. **Foreign income of a Nigerian resident is taxable in Nigeria**, and platforms increasingly report. Nothing here makes holding a domiciliary account or investing abroad unlawful; it makes doing it undocumented unwise. Keep records of every inflow.

1. **Open a domiciliary account before you have dollars.** The account takes days.
2. **Write down your target hard-currency percentage** and the date you will rebalance to it. Unwritten targets drift.
3. **Convert every naira return into dollars once a year**, just to see it. The exercise is unpleasant and it is the most clarifying thing in this book.


**In plain words:** Much of what you will buy is priced in dollars — medicine, phones, cars, school abroad. If all your savings are in naira and the naira falls, you can earn a good return and still afford less than before. Keep part of your savings in the currency you will actually spend. A 5% dollar return is not worse than a 15% naira return; they are different currencies.

**If you remember one thing:** match currency to what you will actually spend it on — a 5% dollar return and a 15% naira return are not comparable numbers, they are different units.

**In Pidgin:** If na abroad you go spend the money, keep am for that money. 5% dollar no be less than 15% naira — na two different money.
## 28. Gold, crypto, and other things that do not feed you

A category defined by a shared property: **these assets produce nothing.** No dividend, no interest, no rent, no harvest. Their entire return depends on someone paying more later. That does not make them useless — it makes them a different kind of thing, and the error is treating them as the same kind of thing.

**Gold.** Priced in dollars globally, so for a Nigerian it hedges twice: gold's own price and the naira's decline. Routes: the **NewGold ETF** on the NGX, backed by physical gold, bought through your stockbroker — the simplest regulated route, with modest volumes; or physical gold, where you pay a large dealer margin buying and take another haircut selling, and jewellery carries a craftsmanship premium you will never recover.

**Crypto**, and I intend to be straight about this because most commentary is either evangelism or contempt.

Nigeria has among the world's highest adoption rates and **the reason is not speculation.** It is that a person holding a falling currency wants dollars, and USDT is dollars that arrive in ten minutes on a Sunday, without a form. That is a real use case, produced by a real problem, and anyone who dismisses it has not tried to move money in this country.

So, precisely:

- **Stablecoins are a payment and preservation tool, not an investment.** They pay nothing. If someone offers you yield on a stablecoin you have taken on credit risk with an entity you cannot assess — return to Chapter 14. Your risks here are the issuer and the platform, not price.
- **Bitcoin and the rest are speculative.** Falls of 70% have happened repeatedly and will again. A small allocation you could watch go to zero is defensible. Your emergency fund, your rent, and borrowed money are not.
- **The regulation has moved substantially:** the CBN's 2021 restriction on banks servicing exchanges was lifted at the end of 2023 with a licensing framework, and the Investments and Securities Act 2025 brought digital assets under SEC oversight. This area changes faster than anything else in this book — check the current position and prefer platforms inside the framework.
- **"Crypto" is the current costume of the Ponzi.** CBEX was not a crypto investment; it was Chapter 14 with a dashboard. Every question there applies with extra force to anything advertising an "AI trading bot."
- **Self-custody means self-responsibility.** Lose the seed phrase and it is gone permanently — no bank, no regulator, no appeal. Never store one in a photograph, an email or a chat.
- **P2P trading carries a specific Nigerian risk:** receiving proceeds of fraud into your bank account, and a frozen account. Use platforms with escrow and keep records.

**Commodities**, briefly: Nigeria produces cocoa, sesame, cashew, ginger, maize. Exchanges such as AFEX offer warehouse receipts and commodity-linked instruments. Real, regulated, and specialist — storage, grading and logistics dominate the outcome. Not a starting point.

> Assets that produce nothing belong in small sizes, held for what they protect you against, not for what they might do. The moment you start expecting them to make you rich, you have changed the position from insurance into a bet, without changing the size.

**What to do.**

**The law on this.** The position changed decisively. The CBN's 2021 restriction on banks servicing crypto businesses was lifted at the end of 2023 with a licensing framework, and the **Investments and Securities Act 2025** brought **digital assets within the definition of securities**, so platforms dealing in them require SEC registration. Gains on digital assets are within the tax net. Separately, the **Money Laundering (Prevention and Prohibition) Act 2022** means large or unusual flows are reported, and receiving fraud proceeds through peer-to-peer trading is how innocent people get accounts frozen. Use registered platforms and keep records of every trade.

1. **Cap this entire category** — gold, crypto, commodities — at something you would not mourn. For most people 5% to 10% of investable assets.
2. **If you use stablecoins for payments, treat them as a wallet, not a portfolio**, and never accept yield offers on them.
3. **Keep records of every purchase and disposal.** Gains on digital assets are within the tax net.


**In plain words:** Gold and crypto produce nothing. No rent, no interest, no dividend. Their whole return depends on someone paying more later. Keep them small — money you could watch go to zero. Stablecoins are useful for holding dollar value and moving money, but they pay you nothing, and anyone offering you profit on them is offering you a risk you cannot see.

**If you remember one thing:** assets that produce nothing belong in small sizes, held for what they protect you against; the moment you expect them to make you rich you have changed the bet without changing the size.

**In Pidgin:** Anything wey no dey produce anything, keep am small. And any 'AI trading bot' na the same old story with new cloth.
## 29. Ajo, esusu, and the wisdom that survived

> *Sannu ba ta hana zuwa.* — **Hausa:** going slowly does not prevent arrival.

I want to close this Book with the oldest instrument in it, because it teaches something the newest ones cannot.

**Ajo, esusu, adashe** — a rotating savings arrangement. A group contributes a fixed amount at fixed intervals; each period one member takes the pot; it rotates until everyone has collected. It has operated in West Africa for centuries, survived colonisation, several currencies, a civil war, six military governments, structural adjustment, and the arrival of formal banking — which mostly failed to displace it.

**Anything that has survived that long deserves respect before it deserves analysis.** Things that have lasted a long time have usually been tested against dangers that the people who designed the newer alternatives never encountered. A financial arrangement that has worked through a currency collapse has passed an examination that no bank's risk model has sat.

**What ajo actually provides**, stated properly:

- **Enforced discipline**, through social obligation rather than willpower. This is not a small thing; willpower has an appalling track record, and the arrangement outsources it to a group of people who will ask you in person.
- **Access to a lump sum** without a loan, without interest, without collateral, without a credit assessment.
- **No counterparty risk at all** — provided the group is people you actually know, and provided the collector is not an individual holding everyone's money.

**What it does not provide, and this is where honesty is required:**

- **Any return.** You get back roughly what you put in. Under 22% inflation, money waiting its turn in a rotating scheme is losing value the whole time. It is a savings *discipline*, not an investment.
- **Protection against default** by someone who collects and then stops contributing.
- **Recourse** if an individual "ajo collector" disappears with the deposits — which happens regularly and is the main way this ancient, sound structure gets corrupted, by inserting a single point of failure into a design that never had one.

**Its formal descendant, the registered cooperative society, is superior in almost every way** and criminally under-used by people who are eligible. Registered with the state, with a constitution, elected officers and audited accounts. Savings earn a share of the surplus; **loans come at rates dramatically below commercial banks — often single digits — usually up to a multiple of your savings, repaid by salary deduction.** Many run housing and land schemes buying in bulk.

**If your workplace has a cooperative, join it this month.** For a salaried Nigerian it is frequently the cheapest borrowing available anywhere, and — see Chapter 8 — cheap long money in an inflationary country is one of the few genuine gifts the system offers.

**What to check before joining anything of this kind:** is it registered; are accounts audited and circulated to members; who can authorise withdrawals; how are officers elected; and how long have the same people been holding the money? An unregistered "cooperative" run by one charismatic person with no accounts is Chapter 14 in traditional dress, and the traditional dress makes it harder to see.

**Mama Nkechi still runs her ajo**, thirty-one years in, with some of the same women. When I asked her why — she has a money market account now, and the fund pays her something while the ajo pays her nothing — she looked at me as though I had asked why she still eats.

*"If I no drop, dem go come ask me for my shop. Fund no dey ask person anything."*

That is the entire argument for keeping it, stated better than I have managed in four pages. **The ajo is not competing with the fund. The ajo is the reason there is anything to put in the fund.** What changed for her was not the ajo; it was where the pot goes on the day it arrives.

**What to do.**

1. **Use ajo to force savings if you need forcing** — then move each lump sum immediately into something that earns.
2. **Join your workplace cooperative** and find out your borrowing entitlement before you ever need it.
3. **Never join a scheme where one individual holds the money and there are no accounts**, however traditional the form or respected the person.

**In plain words:** Ajo forces you to save, because people will ask you in person. That is worth something no app can give. But it pays you nothing while your money waits, so the day your pot arrives, move it into a fund that same day. And never join one where a single person holds everybody's money with no accounts.

**If you remember one thing:** keep the ajo for the discipline it supplies, and move the pot into a regulated instrument the day it arrives.

**In Pidgin:** Keep the ajo, because e dey force you save. But the day pot enter your hand, carry am go put for fund same day.
## 30. Amana: money without interest, and trust as capital

For a great many Nigerian readers, the instruments in the last twelve chapters carry a problem that no yield can solve: most of them pay **riba**, and riba is forbidden.

I am not going to treat this as a footnote for a minority, because it is not. It is the frame through which tens of millions of Nigerians — the majority in much of the North, and substantial communities everywhere else — must read every chapter of this book. A financial book that mentions non-interest finance once, in a table, has quietly told a third of the country that it was written for somebody else.

So this chapter does two things. It builds a **complete non-interest version of everything this book has recommended** — floor, growth, insurance and all. And it examines the commercial system that has operated in northern Nigeria for centuries without any of these instruments, because it contains a mechanism the rest of the country underuses.

---

**First, the intellectual point, which surprised me when I worked through it.**

The Islamic prohibition is usually explained to outsiders as a rule about interest. It is broader and more interesting than that. Three things are prohibited: **riba** (a guaranteed return on money merely for lending it), **gharar** (excessive uncertainty in a contract's terms), and **maysir** (gambling). What is required in their place is that the financier **share the outcome** and that the arrangement be **tied to a real asset**.

Read that list again with the previous twenty-four chapters in mind.

A prohibition on guaranteed returns for taking no risk is Chapter 11 — skin in the game — expressed as law rather than as advice. A requirement that contracts be asset-backed rather than abstract is a structural defence against the layered, opaque paper that fails catastrophically in every crisis. And a prohibition on gambling is Chapter 5, written down eleven centuries earlier.

> A rule that forbids taking a fixed return while bearing no share of the loss is not a religious inconvenience attached to finance. It is a solution to the principal-agent problem, and it predates the literature by a millennium.

I say this as an observation about mechanism, not as theology, on which I have no standing. But a reader who has been told that non-interest finance is a constraint should consider the possibility that it is, in this particular respect, a superior default.

---

### The non-interest floor

Chapter 34's barbell requires a large safe end. Nearly everything I have used to build it — treasury bills, money market funds, bank deposits, bonds — pays interest. Here is the same structure, built without it.

| The interest-bearing version | The non-interest equivalent |
|---|---|
| Money market fund | A **non-interest bank** current or savings account (profit-sharing rather than interest), or a **Shariah-compliant money market or fixed-income fund** run by a Nigerian manager under a Shariah advisory board |
| Treasury bills / FGN Bonds | **FGN Sukuk** — asset-backed, paying a share of rental income from named road projects rather than interest. Retail minimums have been modest, commonly around ₦10,000 |
| Corporate bonds | **Corporate sukuk**, issued by a small number of Nigerian companies and states |
| Equity fund / ETF | A **Shariah-screened equity fund**, or the **halal equity ETF** listed on the NGX; the **NGX Lotus Islamic Index** is the screened benchmark |
| Pension Fund II or III | **Fund VI** — PenCom's non-interest fund, in both active and retiree variants. It exists; you must ask for it |
| Conventional insurance | **Takaful** — mutual risk-sharing rather than risk-transfer, offered by NAICOM-licensed operators, including family (life) and general takaful |
| Dollar savings | A domiciliary account, plus gold (the NewGold ETF is asset-backed), plus dollar sukuk where available |

**Non-interest banks** operating in Nigeria have included Jaiz Bank, TAJBank and Lotus Bank, along with non-interest windows at conventional banks and a set of non-interest microfinance banks. As always: verify the current licence with the CBN yourself rather than taking my word, and apply Chapter 14's checklist regardless of how the institution presents itself. **A religious label is not a substitute for a licence**, and the schemes in Chapter 14 have used Islamic framing as readily as Christian.

**A word on the honest disagreement.** Not every scholar accepts every product marketed as compliant, and some structures replicate a fixed return through a chain of asset transactions that critics regard as a formality. I am not qualified to arbitrate this and will not pretend to be. **Ask for the Shariah advisory board's name and its published pronouncement, read the structure, and take your own scholarly advice** — which is, incidentally, the same procedure I recommend for every other instrument in this book: understand the mechanism rather than the label.

---

### Zakat is a planning number, and nobody plans for it

Here is the practical consequence that gets omitted from every financial book written in Lagos.

**Zakat is an annual obligation of 2.5% on qualifying wealth held above the nisab threshold for a lunar year** — on savings, gold, silver, cash, trade goods and investments held for gain, though not typically on your home, your tools or your personal effects.

Now put that into Chapter 8's arithmetic. Your money market equivalent returns 19%. Inflation is 23%. And 2.5% of the *stock* leaves annually.

**Your real hurdle rate is not inflation. It is inflation plus zakat.** A Muslim investor needs a higher gross return than a non-Muslim to stand in the same place, and it is rarely stated plainly, with the result that zakat is paid out of whatever is convenient in the month it falls due rather than provided for.

**Treat it as a scheduled liability, exactly like school fees:**

1. **Fix your zakat date** — the same date each lunar year — so the calculation is regular rather than improvised.
2. **Compute across all assets**, not only the bank account. Trade stock, investment holdings, gold, cash at home and receivables you expect to collect all count; debts you owe are generally deductible. Get the calculation right with someone qualified once, then repeat the method.
3. **Provide for it monthly.** Divide last year's zakat by twelve and move that amount into a separate non-interest account each month. The obligation then arrives funded, and you never sell an asset badly to meet it.
4. **Note the effect on asset choice.** Because zakat falls on *held wealth* rather than on income, it quietly favours productive assets over idle balances — a large cash pile is charged every year, while the tools of a working business generally are not. That is an incentive pointing in the same direction as this entire book.

**Sadaqah**, being voluntary, belongs in the ceremonial-calendar budget of Chapter 46 alongside every other giving obligation — planned as a line, not improvised under pressure.

---

### Amana: what the northern trade networks solved

Now the mechanism, and I offer it the same way I offered ajo in Chapter 29 — as a structure with strengths and failure modes, not as a cultural ornament.

Long-distance trade across the Sahel and into the forest belt has operated for centuries on credit, without enforceable contracts, functioning courts at either end, or any of the apparatus a modern financier would consider indispensable. Grain moves from Kano; livestock moves south; goods, currency and obligation move on trust between people who may meet twice a year.

**The collateral in that system is reputation, and the word for it is amana — trust, or something held in trust.** A trader's standing is his balance sheet. Default is not primarily a legal event but a social one: the defaulter is not sued, he is cut off, and being cut off from the network ends the business more thoroughly than any judgment would.

**Why this works, mechanically:** it converts a one-off transaction into a repeated game. Cheating once wins a single consignment and forfeits every future one. Where the network is dense and information travels, this enforcement is *cheaper and faster than courts* — which is precisely why it grew in places where courts were distant.

**Where it fails, and this matters:**

- **It does not scale to strangers.** The system's strength is that everybody's history is known. Introduce participants with no history — as urbanisation, migration and the internet all do — and the enforcement mechanism evaporates while the habit of trusting remains. **That gap is where fraud enters**, and it is why Chapter 14's schemes travel so effectively along networks built for a different era.
- **Reputation is illiquid.** Thirty years of standing cannot be pledged to a bank, sold, or left to your children. A trader with excellent amana and no documents cannot obtain a ₦20 million facility that his creditworthiness plainly justifies.
- **It is fragile to shocks that hit everyone at once.** A drought, a border closure, a currency collapse or an insurgency does not distinguish honest defaulters from dishonest ones, and a system of mutual credit can unwind in a season.

**And one specific instrument worth naming, because it is real capital treated as invisible: livestock.** Cattle have functioned across northern Nigeria as savings — a store of value that reproduces, resists inflation, and can be converted quickly. It is a genuine asset class with a genuine risk register: disease, drought, theft, rustling and insecurity, none of which is diversifiable within the herd. **A family whose entire savings walk about on four legs in a region with a security problem is running Chapter 10's concentration risk in its purest form** — which is the argument for moving some portion into instruments that cannot be driven away, exactly as I argued in Chapter 31 that money must leave the trader's shop.

**The synthesis I would offer**, and it applies as much in Onitsha and Balogun as in Kano: **the network gives you what no institution can — cheap enforcement, fast credit, and standing. The institutions give you what the network cannot — documentation, scale, portability and survival past the death of the people who know you.** Use both. Register the business, hold the accounts, keep the papers — and keep the amana, because in the year the documents fail you it is the network that will still be there.

**What to do.**

1. **If interest is not available to you, build the same barbell with the right-hand column of the table above** — sukuk and non-interest deposits as the floor, a Shariah-screened equity fund and your own business as the convex end, takaful for protection, Fund VI in your pension.
2. **Ask your PFA for Fund VI explicitly.** Like Fund I, it is opt-in, and nobody will move you.
3. **Fix a zakat date, compute it properly once with someone qualified, and provide for it monthly.** It is a real annual charge on your wealth and it belongs in your plan rather than in your surprises.
4. **Whatever your standing in a trade network, put the documents in place anyway** — CAC registration, a bank account, records. Reputation cannot be inherited; a registered business can.
5. **Apply Chapter 14 to non-interest products exactly as to any other.** A Shariah board is a mechanism to examine, not a reason to skip the SEC register.


**In plain words:** If your faith does not permit interest, you can still build everything in this book. Use sukuk instead of treasury bills, a non-interest bank, Fund VI in your pension, a screened equity fund, and takaful instead of ordinary insurance. And remember that zakat takes 2.5% of your wealth each year, so plan for it monthly like school fees.

**If you remember one thing:** the prohibition on riba is not an obstacle to be worked around but a structure with the same logic as this book — and a complete non-interest floor exists, so build it, and remember that zakat is 2.5% of your wealth every year and belongs in the plan.

**In Pidgin:** If interest no be your way, plenty option still dey — sukuk, non-interest bank, Fund VI, halal fund, takaful. And zakat na part of your planning, no be surprise.
## 31. The market without an employer

> *Onye aghala nwanne ya.* — **Igbo:** let no one abandon his kin. The sentence the apprenticeship system was built on.

Most working Nigerians have no employer. Estimates put the informal share of employment above eighty percent — traders, tailors, mechanics, drivers, farmers, hairdressers, welders, food sellers, freelancers, the entire vast middle of the economy that produces most of the country's actual work and none of its financial products.

Nearly all financial writing is addressed to the salaried minority. This chapter is not.

**The four problems**, stated plainly, because they are structural and not moral:

1. **No employer contributes anything.** No pension match, no group life cover, no HMO.
2. **Income is irregular** — good weeks, dead weeks, seasons. Every "save ₦50,000 monthly" instruction fails on contact with this.
3. **Business money and household money are the same money**, physically, in the same place, so profitability is unknowable and so is saving.
4. **Records live in one phone**, which can be lost, stolen, or seized.

Each has a practical answer.

---

**Problem one: the pension nobody told you about.**

The **Micro Pension Plan** exists precisely for you, and take-up remains very low. It is regulated by PenCom, the assets are held by a custodian separate from the administrator, and it invests in the same instruments as the formal-sector money.

*Step by step:*

1. **Choose a PFA.** Any of them; compare published fund returns over three to five years.
2. **Walk into a branch or use the app.** Bring **BVN, NIN, a valid ID, a passport photograph** and your bank details.
3. **You receive an RSA PIN** — your permanent pension identity. Write it in the master document (Chapter 50).
4. **Contribute anything, whenever.** ₦2,000 after a good market day. ₦20,000 after a good season. There is no schedule and no penalty for missing one, which is the entire point.
5. **Understand the split.** A portion is designated **contingent** and may be withdrawn before retirement under the rules; the rest is locked for retirement. Confirm the current proportions and withdrawal conditions with the PFA, as the framework has been revised since launch.
6. **It is yours and portable.** It does not depend on any employer, association, cooperative or town union continuing to exist.

**Why bother, when you have the business?** Because the business is one asset, in one place, exposed to one set of risks, run by one person — you — who will eventually become old or ill. The pension is the part of your wealth that is not the business. Chapter 10 is the argument; this is the instrument.

**Problem two: irregular income needs a percentage, not an amount.**

Do not attempt "₦50,000 monthly." It will work twice and then break, and the breaking will convince you that saving is not for people like you.

Instead: **save a percentage of every good day.** Ten or twenty percent of takings, moved the same day. On a dead day you save nothing and you have broken no promise. This is the only savings rule I have seen survive contact with irregular income, and it works because it never asks you for money you do not have.

Then add two structures:

- **A season fund.** Every trade has lean months — after school resumption, after Christmas, during the rains, during a fuel scarcity. Compute what a lean month costs you and hold that many months in a money market fund. This is your emergency fund, and for a trader it needs to be larger than the salaried person's, not smaller.
- **A peak-season sweep.** After your best month — December for many traders, harvest for farmers, resumption for uniform sellers — move a defined lump into something that cannot be spent casually: an FGN Savings Bond (₦5,000 minimum, quarterly interest, two or three years) or a Treasury bill. **The point is not the yield. The point is that it is inconvenient to reach**, and inconvenience is the cheapest self-control device ever invented.

**Problem three: separate the money, physically.**

This is the single highest-return act in this chapter and it costs nothing.

**Two accounts. One for the business, one for the household.** Every sale goes into the business account. You pay yourself a fixed "salary" from it into the household account, on a fixed day. The household spends only from the household account.

What this gives you immediately: you can see whether the business is profitable, which most informal operators genuinely cannot; you can see how much you actually spend; you have a bank record, which is the beginning of ever getting formal credit at 15% instead of 400%; and you stop financing a slow month by quietly eating your stock.

**Register the business with CAC** and get a TIN. It costs little, it takes days, and it unlocks a business bank account, corporate customers, grant schemes, government programmes and — critically for Chapter 43 — an asset that is legally yours and can be inherited.

**Problem four: records that survive a lost phone.**

- **Keep a physical notebook.** One line per day: takings, major purchases, debts owed to you, debts you owe. Rough is fine. Nothing beats a notebook for surviving a phone theft, a dead battery, or a network outage.
- **Photograph the notebook weekly and email it to yourself.** That is a backup, and it costs one minute.
- **Write one page listing every account you have** — bank, MMF, RSA PIN, cooperative, insurance, and who owes you money — and give a copy to one person you trust. This is the master document of Chapter 50, and for a trader whose wealth is stock and receivables rather than certificates, it matters more, not less.

---

**Combining ajo with the regulated world, without breaking what works.**

Do not abandon your ajo. It supplies something no fund can: **social enforcement.** People will ask you in person, and that works where willpower does not.

But ajo pays nothing while your money waits its turn, and under 22% inflation the waiting is expensive (Chapter 29). So:

> Keep the ajo for the discipline. Move the pot the day it arrives.

The moment your collection comes, that same day, it goes into the money market fund, the savings bond, or the pension. Not next week. The pot has a half-life measured in hours once it is sitting in your hands and your relatives know it arrived.

And if your ajo is run by a single individual holding everyone's money rather than by a rotating group of people you know — leave. That is not tradition; that is a single point of failure inserted into a design that never had one, and it is how a sound thousand-year-old structure gets converted into Chapter 14.

**Convex experiments that fit irregular cash flow.**

The trader's version of Chapter 41, and it is the one most Nigerians already do instinctively:

- **A new product line in small quantity.** ₦20,000 of something you have not sold before. Most will do nothing. One may become half your revenue. Cost bounded, payoff unbounded.
- **A second market day, or a second location tested for a month** before any commitment.
- **One customer segment you have never served** — an office, a school, a church, a delivery arrangement through a phone.
- **A skill added in the slow season**, which costs the time you were losing anyway.

The rule is the same as everywhere in this book: **many small, none fatal, drop what does not work quickly, expand what does.** You do not need to know which one will work. You need enough attempts and small enough losses.

**And the trap specific to trading:** reinvesting one hundred percent of profit into stock, forever. It feels like growth and it is concentration. **Some money must leave the business every single month** — into the pension, into the fund, into the bond. The trader who has ₦8 million of goods and ₦0 outside them is one fire, one seizure, one illness away from starting again at fifty.

### Case: Mama Nkechi, eleven years of a ₦3,000 ajo

*Composite; names invented.*

This is Mama Nkechi from the Prologue, and this is what the eleven years actually looked like from the inside.

In 2014 she was contributing ₦3,000 weekly to an ajo of twelve women in Balogun, collecting about ₦36,000 every twelve weeks, which reliably disappeared into stock, school fees and family obligations within a fortnight. She had been trading for nineteen years and had, in her own description, "nothing to show" — by which she meant no money anywhere that was not either cloth or somebody's school fees.

Three changes, over eleven years, none of them dramatic.

**In 2015 she opened a second bank account** and began paying every day's takings into it, drawing a fixed ₦25,000 weekly for the household on Sundays. Within four months she knew, for the first time in nineteen years of trading, whether the shop was profitable. It was, less than she had assumed.

**In 2017 she stopped spending the ajo pot.** She kept the ajo — the women expected her, and that expectation was doing work no app could — but each collection went the same day into a money market fund a neighbour's daughter had helped her open. She was not persuaded by the yield; she was persuaded that the money took four days to come back, which meant relatives could not reach it on a Tuesday.

**In 2019 she opened a Micro Pension account** and began paying in ₦2,000 after good market days, irregularly, some months not at all.

She also, in 2018, spent ₦40,000 stocking a product she had not sold before — children's school socks — on a hunch about a nearby school's uniform policy. It failed. She tried three other lines over the following years; one, phone accessories, became about a third of her revenue by 2023.

By 2025 she had a money market balance of about ₦4.1 million, a pension account, a shop, and a daughter in her second year at university without a loan. She could not tell you what a money market fund invests in.

**What she did that most traders do not:** she separated the money, she moved the pot the day it arrived, she made money leave the business every month, and she ran four small experiments where one worked. **None of it required a forecast, and none of it required more income than she already had.**

**What to do.**

**The law on this.** The **Micro Pension Plan** operates under the same **Pension Reform Act 2014** as formal-sector pensions, with the same custodian protection — you are not in a lesser scheme. Registering your business under **CAMA 2020** as a business name or company is what turns your trade into something that can be inherited, sold or financed. And if your trade is in a listed category — estate agency, dealing in cars or jewellery, and similar — you may be required to register with **SCUML** under money-laundering law. Chapters 63 and 76 cover registration.

1. **Open a Micro Pension RSA this month.** BVN, NIN, ID, photograph. Contribute ₦5,000 to begin.
2. **Open a second bank account today and separate business from household money.** Pay yourself a fixed amount on a fixed day.
3. **Save a percentage of every good day, not a fixed monthly sum.**
4. **Move every ajo pot into a regulated instrument the day it arrives.**
5. **Register with CAC and get a TIN** — it is the door to everything formal, including credit that does not cost 400%.

**If you remember one thing:** the business is not the plan; the business is one asset. Money must leave it every month and go somewhere the business cannot reach.


**In plain words:** Most Nigerians have no employer. If that is you: open a Micro Pension account, keep business money in one account and household money in another, pay yourself a fixed amount weekly, and save a share of every good day rather than a fixed monthly sum. Some money must leave the business every month and go where the business cannot reach it.

**In Pidgin:** The shop no be the plan — the shop na one property. Money must comot from am every month go where the shop no fit reach.
## 32. The silent partner: tax

You have a partner in every venture. He contributes no capital, does no work, takes a share of every profit, and takes nothing when you lose. He is entitled to it, he is not going away, and most Nigerians manage him by pretending he is not there — which is the one strategy guaranteed to be expensive.

> Tax has a concave payoff: it participates in your gains and not in your losses. Since you cannot remove it, the only remaining move is to place your money where it takes least, legally.

Nigeria's tax system was substantially rewritten in 2025, with the new legislation taking effect from 2026. **This chapter is therefore a map of what to ask about, not a schedule of current rates.** Verify with FIRS, your state revenue service, or an adviser before acting on any number here.

**Personal income tax.** Charged on individuals at graduated rates and administered by **your state's** revenue service, based on residence — not by FIRS, which handles a few defined categories. The 2025 reforms raised the threshold at which people begin to pay, exempting the lowest earners entirely, revised the bands, and replaced parts of the old relief structure — notably introducing a **rent relief** in place of some of the previous consolidated allowance. If you are salaried, PAYE is deducted for you; if you are self-employed, **you are supposed to file, and increasingly you will need to have done so** — a tax clearance certificate is required for property transactions, government contracts, some visas and various licences.

**Capital gains.** The big change, and the one that affects readers of this book.

- **Shares.** For many years, gains on Nigerian share disposals were effectively exempt. Under the 2025 reforms they became chargeable **above defined thresholds** — with exemptions tied to the size of disposal proceeds in a twelve-month period and to the size of the gain, and with **relief where the proceeds are reinvested** in Nigerian shares. The thresholds are the thing to check, and the reinvestment relief is the thing to understand *before* you sell rather than after.
- **Property.** Gains on disposal are chargeable, with an exemption that has generally applied to a dwelling house used as your main residence. Verify the current form of that exemption before selling a family home.
- **Digital assets.** Within the net. Keep purchase and disposal records; you will need the acquisition cost.

**Withholding tax.** Deducted at source. On **dividends** it is commonly 10% and treated as **final** — nothing further to pay, which is why a "10% dividend yield" is 9% in your hand and why the arithmetic in Chapter 20 should always be done after tax.

**The transaction taxes**, which people forget and which are large in property:

- **Stamp duty** on documents and instruments, including deeds and share transfers.
- **Registration fees** at the land registry.
- **Governor's Consent fees**, which are percentage-based and vary by state, and which are frequently the largest single line in a property purchase after the price (Chapter 26).
- **Land use charge** and ground rent, annually, for as long as you hold it.
- **VAT** on the fees your broker, lawyer and agent charge you.

Together these are the "perfection cost" that turns an advertised 40% property gain into something considerably smaller. **Compute them before you buy, not when you sell.**

---

**Now the part that is genuinely under-discussed, and which belongs in a book about inflation.**

**You are taxed on nominal gains, not real ones.**

Suppose you buy an asset for ₦10,000,000 and sell it three years later for ₦17,000,000. On paper: a ₦7,000,000 gain. But if inflation ran at 20% a year, ₦10,000,000 in year zero is worth about ₦17,280,000 in year-three money. **In real terms you lost.** You are nonetheless taxed on the ₦7,000,000, because the tax code counts naira and not purchasing power.

This is not a Nigerian peculiarity — most tax systems do it — but its severity scales with inflation, and at Nigerian inflation rates it is severe. **At high inflation, capital gains tax becomes partly a tax on the currency's decline**, charged to the person who was trying to escape it.

The same mechanism operates on your salary. **Bracket creep:** the bands are written in naira. Your employer raises your pay 20% to keep you level with inflation. Your real income has not changed at all, but a larger portion of it now falls in a higher band, so your effective tax rate rises. **You are poorer in goods and richer on paper, and you are taxed on the paper.**

Two conclusions follow, and they are practical:

1. **Instruments with tax advantages are worth more in a high-inflation country than the headline advantage suggests.** Which brings us to the best one available to you.
2. **Holding periods and timing matter.** Where thresholds are annual, spreading a large disposal across tax years may keep you inside an exemption. This is legitimate planning, not evasion, and it requires you to think before you press sell.

**The pension, again.** Contributions reduce taxable income going in; growth is untaxed inside the fund; compliant retirement benefits are generally exempt coming out. **That is a three-way advantage no other Nigerian product offers.** For a salaried person it is the highest-tax-efficiency vehicle available, and it is sitting there being ignored by people who spend evenings looking for a better return.

**Records, which are the whole game.**

Under a capital gains regime, you compute the gain as proceeds minus **cost of acquisition**. If you cannot document what you paid, you may find yourself effectively taxed on the whole proceeds. So:

- **Keep every contract note** from your broker, forever. They are small PDFs.
- **Keep the deed, the receipts, the survey, the consent fees, the agent's invoice.** Perfection costs and improvement costs typically increase your base and reduce your gain — but only if you can prove them.
- **Keep crypto purchase records**, which nobody does and which are the hardest to reconstruct.
- **Get a TIN**, whatever your employment status.

**What to do.**
